  shares with stock. amonet v1.1.0's LK passes, so a part-written chain still
  resumes. When `lk_a` matches neither, `lk_b` is read too: the chain writes
  `lk_a` first, so a run stopped inside that write leaves `lk_a` torn and `lk_b`
  as it was. Both routes into the chain leave amonet v2.0.0's LK in both slots:
  its installer writes both, and so does the fastbrick, measured on a Dot read
  the moment its TWRP came up from stock. Every stock LK is 241664 or 245760
  bytes, against 359744 and 372368, so none can match. The check is defence in
  depth, not a fix for a reachable failure.
- The probe waits for `mtp` on every TWRP, not only amonet v1.1.0's. adb
  answers first, and the switch to `mtp,adb` re-enumerates USB, so a stage
  starting in that window reads truncated output with exit 255. So each
  device-side answer this route reads ends in a marker of its own, `sgdisk
  --print` included, because adb cuts the tail: a marker at the front leaves a
  cut line parsing as an empty, and therefore passing, result. A cut md5 is
  shorter than 32 characters, but a read that returned nothing hashes to a
  whole one, so the chain's reads and writes also require `dd`'s record count.
- v2.0.0's TWRP costs the install nothing. It returns `adb shell`'s exit status
  and has every tool the install needs, and `/system` took 81 s there, as under
  bboe2: `gunzip` and the eMMC are the limit, not adb.
- `misc`'s boot control block is written, as a read of block 1, 7 bytes patched
  at 0x160, and a write back, which is what v1.1.0's `reset_bcb` does. Leaving
  it inherits whatever ran before: after an `amonet-biscuit-v2.0.0` install a
  Dot read `00 41 42 42 01 af 3e`, slot b at prio 14 with 3 tries, a live
  fallback to the Fire OS 6 images an amonet v1.1.0 chain cannot boot. The boot
  image goes to slot a to match, not to `ro.boot.slot_suffix`, which here names
  the slot amonet v2.0.0 booted.
- Measured on a Dot: the timings table above has the figures.

## Downgrade: amonet v2.0.0 to v1.1.0

- firebreak erases boot0, then opens the port whose USB product id is the
  bootrom's, so a port that was already there does not matter.
- The engine is the GCPU, `gcpu@10210000` in the device tree: a 4 KiB window
  that holds every register the exploit touches. Fire OS hands it to the TEE,
  which is encrypted, so the registers are read from the preloader instead.
- The preloader drives the engine with the same sequence, at `0x1d1bc` as
  Thumb-2. It names every `gcpu.Register` and gives `ACQUIRE_VALUES` and
  `SLOT_POINTER_VALUES`. `0x400`, `0xC48` and `0xC68` are computed, not
  literals, so a byte search misses them. The routines are identical in every
  cached preloader, biscuit's and crown's. `tests/test_preloader.py` checks
  them, and skips without a cache, so CI never runs it.
- The bootrom owns `0x102868` and `0x1028A8`, so the preloader does not hold
  them. Both agree with [mtkclient](https://github.com/bkerler/mtkclient)
  (GPL-3.0); nothing was copied. mtkclient also clears `0x1072DC`. One write
  to `0x102868` has been enough on every run, and why is not known.
- This route now runs only from amonet v2.0.0's fastboot, for `--short`. From
  amonet v2.0.0's TWRP the targets write the chain in place instead; the
  section above has it.
- The script stops if `lk_build_desc` is already v1.1.0's: a second erase
  gains nothing. It reads `getvar`, and refuses a Dot whose `unlock_status` is
  not `true`.
- It runs `fastboot erase boot0` and `fastboot reboot`, and the Dot drops into
  its bootrom.
- The step ends with the plan's own reboot. Every write reports its own
  result, so no log is read.
- The bootrom is `0e8d:0003`. On macOS it is `/dev/cu.usbmodem*`. On Linux it
  is `/dev/ttyACM*`, owned by `dialout` unless the udev `tty` line gives it to
  `plugdev`, and ModemManager can grab it first. On Windows it is a COM port
  that needs MediaTek's VCOM driver (`cdc-acm.inf`, class Ports, 3.0.1504.0,
  "MediaTek USB Port" in the Microsoft Update Catalog), installed by hand
  with `pnputil /add-driver`; without it `0e8d:0003` is an unknown device.
- No other mode needs a hand-installed driver. On a Windows machine that rooted
  a Dot, the driver store held only MediaTek's and Amazon's
  `FireDevicesUsbDeviceClass`, which serves amonet fastboot (`0bb4:0c01`) and
  can come through Windows Update. Windows bound adb (`1949:0110` and Fire
  OS 6's `0e8d:201c`, by its interface class through its own `winusb.inf`) and
  v1.1.0's TWRP (`18d1:4ee2`) itself. TWRP 3.7.0_9-bboeN is untried on
  Windows. macOS and Linux need no driver.
- A port that will not open is named once and retried: udev or a modem
  manager can hold it for a moment. The wait is 60 seconds after the erase,
  and 600 when the run waits for a short.
- The run uploads v2.0.0's payload from the fastbrick's zip, reads the Dot's
  partition table over USB, resolves v1.1.0's plan against it, and reads every
  write back. On macOS and Windows the step takes 18 s.
- The bootrom's word commands are big-endian and the Dot's memory is
  little-endian, so the payload and the AES input go out byte-swapped per
  word. Unswapped bytes load in the wrong order and nothing on the wire shows
  it, which is why `write_words` takes words.
- It reads and writes 64 blocks per command, `0x1004` and `0x1003`. One block
  per command was 10 times slower: every region is read twice, and each round
  trip costs about 10 ms.
- The payload does not answer the area switch, `0x1002`, so block 0 is checked
  after each one: boot0 starts `EMMC_BOOT` or is zeros, and the user area ends
  `55 AA`. A read-back alone cannot catch a lost switch, because it reads
  through the same wrong area.
- A read-back proves the transfer, not that the eMMC kept the bytes: its
  256 KiB write cache answers reads. The cache is off at power-on and the
  payload never turns it on, so this step writes uncached.
- v2.0.0's payload ends a 512-byte block read and a 256-byte RPMB read on a
  full packet, and Windows' VCOM driver waits for a short packet. So each such
  read is followed by a 4-byte read, `0x5000`, whose bytes are dropped.
- v1.1.0's `fastboot-step.sh` ships a Linux-only fastboot. The script runs its
  three commands with the host's fastboot instead: `bin/tz.img` to `tee2`,
  TWRP 3.7.0_9-bboe2 to `recovery` in place of v1.1.0's `bin/twrp.img`, then
  `fastboot oem reboot-recovery`.

## Install: TWRP 3.7.0_9-bboe2

- The image is the `biscuit` build of
  [bboe/twrp_device_amazon_echo-mt8163](https://github.com/bboe/twrp_device_amazon_echo-mt8163),
  on [bboe/android_kernel_amazon_biscuit](https://github.com/bboe/android_kernel_amazon_biscuit).
  GitHub Actions builds and attests both. The script pins the SHA-256.
- bboe2 adds v1.1.0's TWRP 3.2.3 links: `/dev/block/other-boot` and
  `other-system` name the current slot's kernel and system, and `other-lk` is
  `/dev/null`. EchoMuse's installer finds the kernel through `other-boot`, and
  stopped on bboe1 without it. Measured on a Dot: `other-boot` was
  `mmcblk0p10` and `other-system` `mmcblk0p13`, and both followed a slot
  change.
- v1.1.0's TWRP 3.2.3 moved 5.1 MB/s over adb, and 7.0 MB/s with cpu0 at
  `performance`. This one moves 21.5 to 22.2 MB/s with either governor.
- v2.0.0's TWRP cannot replace 3.2.3. Its kernel is 32-bit, and v1.1.0's LK
  starts only a 64-bit one.
- The kernel is Amazon's 5.5.5.4 one with two back-ports for Android 9's
  `init`. Without SELinux ioctl permissions, `init` fails with `avtab: invalid
  type or class`. Without `mmap_rnd_bits`, it fails with `Unable to set
  adequate mmap entropy value!`.
- amonet v1.1.0 keeps its image in `boot_a` and `boot_b`. Fire OS boots from
  `boot_a_x` and `boot_b_x`. This build points `/boot` at `_x`. The script
  does not rely on that. It writes `boot<slot>_x` and `system<slot>` by name.
- `dd` to a by-name path that does not exist writes a file in TWRP's RAM
  `/dev`, and the read-back of that file matches. So each target must be a
  block device.
- A Dot in TWRP 3.2.3 gets this TWRP written to `recovery` and checked by
  md5. Then the script runs `adb reboot recovery`. The script drops 3.2.3's
  `__bionic_open_tzdata...` lines from `adb shell` output.
- adb answers before MTP is on, as `18d1:d001` in this TWRP. The switch to
  `mtp,adb` puts the Dot back on USB under a new ID. In 3.2.3 the switch
  failed a push with `failed to read copy response: EOF`. So the run waits
  for `mtp` in `sys.usb.config`, and each push gets 3 tries.
- This TWRP's `adb shell` returns the exit status. So each device-side step
  is a pushed script, judged by its status. The scripts have `\n` line
  endings.
- The userdata script unmounts `/sdcard` and `/data`. It formats userdata
  with `mke2fs -t ext4 -b 4096 <userdata> <blocks - 256>`, which leaves 1 MiB
  for the crypto footer. Then it mounts it with `mount -t ext4`. The script
  sets the size, block size and type itself, so the result does not depend on
  the TWRP's fstab.
- `/data` is then checked as a mountpoint. Otherwise Magisk's database lands
  in TWRP's RAM and is gone after the reboot.
- This TWRP mounts system at `/system_root`. So the script patches Fire OS's
  system at `/tmp/fireos-system`.
- A failed patch leaves that mount. So the write first unmounts both.
  `/proc/mounts` keeps the name `mount` was given. So the check looks for the
  by-name link and the `mmcblk0p` node.
- Never `umount -a` in TWRP: it unmounts `/proc`.
- Every push is read back by md5 on the device.

## Writing Fire OS

- The OTA zip's installer does 2 writes that matter: `system.new.dat` to
  `other-system` and `boot.img` to `other-boot`. In TWRP 3.2.3 those name
  the current slot's `system` and `boot_x`. Its LK write goes nowhere:
  amonet's TWRP links `other-lk` to `/dev/null`. Its TEE and preloader are byte
  for byte the ones v1.1.0 already wrote to `tee1` and boot0. Its last write,
  `target.blocklist` to `/cache/recovery/last_blocklist`, is skipped: it is a
  file in `/cache`, not a partition the Dot boots from.
- So the zip never goes to the Dot. The host builds the partition image from
  `system.transfer.list` once, into the cache: 805 MB, 386 MB gzipped. It
  takes 22 s on macOS and 31 s on Windows 11, in a background thread, while
  the Dot unlocks and downgrades.
- The built image matched `system_a` after a `twrp install` in every MiB that
  the root's own edits and ext4's mount metadata leave alone.
- It is built as 128 MiB slices, each its own gzip member, because a
  concatenation of members is one stream to any decompressor. So one cache
  serves both routes.
- Where `adb shell` carries a stream, the slices go out back to back on its
  stdin, `gunzip -c` reads them and `dd` writes the partition, which overlaps
  the transfer with the write and needs no room on the Dot. Where it does not,
  each slice is pushed to `/tmp` and unpacked at its own offset: 35 MB at a
  time against a 240 MiB tmpfs, so nothing is staged on the eMMC.
- The route is chosen by sending 1 KiB holding 0x1a and hashing it on the Dot,
  not by naming an operating system. A probe that does not complete takes the
  pieces, which need no stream, and says so; a probe that arrives cut takes
  them without a word.
- A Windows adb cannot carry it. Measured on bryce in amonet v2.0.0's TWRP,
  from Windows 11 with adb 36.0.1: `gunzip` read 12 bytes and stopped with
  `gzclose: Illegal seek`, having written 12 bytes, while macOS carried 64 MiB
  through the same recovery minutes apart. `adb push` of the same bytes is
  intact from both, at 21.5 MB/s, so the sync protocol is the one that crosses
  hosts. [What adb shell carries](#what-adb-shell-carries) has the byte and
  the counts.
- The command's last line says `stream-ok`, `slice-ok` or `gunzip-failed`, and
  a write is accepted on that line alone, not on silence or on the word
  elsewhere in the output.
- The stream's deadline covers the transfer, not only the wait after it: one
  thread feeds the slices, another reads adb's output, and a stalled adb is
  killed when the 30 minutes run out.
- TWRP 3.2.3 used `adb exec-in`: 65 s on macOS, 76 s on Windows 11. It is
  binary-safe but returns before the Dot has finished, with no status of its
  own, so a read taken when it returns can differ from what lands.
- On a Dot the gzip reaches `cat > /dev/null` in 17.5 s. Through `gunzip`,
  with the output discarded, it takes 36.4 to 39.3 s. So `gunzip` on the Dot
  is the limit. The whole stage, with the eMMC and the md5 read-back, took
  80 s while it streamed, and about 77 s with TWRP 3.2.3. Slicing costs
  23.4 s against 15.9 s for 256 MiB. The sliced route took 108 s to 109 s
  across a whole install from Windows 11.
- The partition is then read back by md5: 12 s with 3.2.3.

## The boot image

- The host builds it from the zip's `boot.img` and Magisk 17.3's zip, in
  about 2 s. Nothing runs magiskboot or Magisk's installer.
- 17.3 is the last 17.x. Up to v25.2 support Android 5.1, but from v18 the
  boot patch and `service.d` path change, and the host's boot image build
  follows 17.3's.
- The ramdisk loses `verify` from every fstab, and `default.prop` gets
  `ro.secure=0`, `ro.debuggable=1` and `persist.sys.usb.config=adb`.
- adb alone, not stock's `mtp`, and not `mtp,adb`. On Ubuntu, GNOME's
  `gvfs-mtp-volume-monitor` reset a booted Dot on `mtp,adb` about every 7 s,
  so the last step's wait for Fire OS 5 timed out; 2 owners hit it. With adb
  alone the Dot has 1 interface, the ADB one, and nothing to probe. Nothing
  here uses MTP, and TWRP's `mtp,adb` is its own ramdisk's.
- The property holds: `init.fosflags.sh`, made to take its adb branch, only
  appends `,adb` where adb is missing, and a fresh root leaves no persisted
  value in `/data/property`. bryce and nether's Dot each read `adb` after a
  reboot, and bryce kept one USB device number for 2 minutes.
- The cmdline is the 512-byte header field at offset 64. Stock is
  `bootopt=64S3,32N2,64N2`; the script appends a space and
  `androidboot.selinux=permissive`.
- The field ends in a NUL, so the line with that addition must stay under 512
  bytes. Padding the field pads but never cuts, so a longer line grew the
  header past its page, moved the kernel and the ramdisk off their offsets,
  and raised nothing. The script stops instead. Biscuit's stock line leaves
  room; this guards another device's.
- Then Magisk's patch, as its `boot_patch.sh` does it with `KEEPVERITY` and
  `KEEPFORCEENCRYPT` false. `init` becomes `magiskinit` and `verity_key`
  goes; both originals go into `.backup`, with `.magisk`. In the kernel,
  `skip_initramfs` becomes `want_initramfs`.
- Magisk's installer also kept a gzipped copy of the image as it found it,
  `/data/stock_boot_<sha1>.img.gz`, and named it in `.backup/.sha1`. Only
  Magisk Manager's image restore reads them, and `stock` is
  the way back to stock here, so neither is written.
- The kernel is a 512-byte MTK header, a gzip stream and the dtb. Only the
  stream and the header's size field change. The ramdisk's cpio is written as
  magiskboot writes one: sorted, inodes from 300000, every mtime 0.
- Reading a cpio ends at the trailer or at the end of the data. A ramdisk cut
  short before its trailer read an empty magic past the end, and the run then
  blamed the format rather than the length.
- The stock image ends in a 2,048-byte signature. It is dropped, as magiskboot
  dropped it: the unlocked LK does not check it.
- Compared with the image that `twrp install` of Magisk wrote on a Dot, every
  header field, the kernel, the dtb and every ramdisk file are the same, except
  in 3 places. There is no `.backup/.sha1`. 3 symlinks keep their stock modes,
  where busybox's `cpio` had made them 0777. The MTK header keeps the stock
  0xff padding, where magiskboot wrote zeros.
- The image is padded to a 4096-byte multiple and written with `dd`.
  After `sync` the page cache is dropped, and the partition is read back by
  md5 over the image's length.
- The host's copies go in a directory under the cache, not in `%TEMP%`. On
  Windows 11, reading a newly written boot image from `%TEMP%` stalled for
  10.3 s in 10 of 15 trials, and in 0 of 23 trials elsewhere.

## /system

- `/system/etc/init.fosflags.sh` strips adb from the USB configuration on
  every boot after the first. The script forces its `FOS_FLAGS_ADB_ON` branch
  true and neutralizes its `unset_adb_persistent_property` calls, then checks
  both edits.
- The 4 Amazon update hosts go into `/system/etc/hosts` as `127.0.0.1`.

## Magisk

- The zip's installer is not run. Its writes go to the Dot as one cpio
  archive, which TWRP's `cpio` unpacks into `/data`. The files are then read
  back as one md5, over all of them in name order.
- `/data/adb/magisk` gets `arm/`, `common/` and `chromeos/` from the zip, mode
  755, and the installer's busybox: base64 of xz, in `update-binary` as
  `BB_ARM`.
- It also gets `magisk`, which `boot_patch.sh` wrote with
  `magiskinit -x magisk`, and which module installs and `--unlock-blocks` run.
  It is the xz stream in `magiskinit` that unpacks to an ELF. Unpacked on the
  host and by `magiskinit -x` on a Dot, its md5 is the same. `/sbin/magisk.bin`
  differs from it in 62 bytes, which magiskinit randomizes at boot.
- On a new userdata the installer wrote to `/data/magisk`, because
  `/data/adb` did not exist yet. Magisk's daemon moved it to `/data/adb/magisk`
  at the first boot. The script writes there directly.
- The rest of the installer changes nothing on this Dot: there is no
  `/system/addon.d`, no `su` in `/system`, and nothing in `/data` to migrate.
- Its database is seeded so the adb shell gets root without a prompt the Dot
  cannot show: `/data/adb/magisk.db`, mode 600, with
  `policies(uid INT, package_name TEXT, policy INT, until INT, logging INT,
  notification INT)` holding `(2000, 'com.android.shell', 2, 0, 1, 0)`.

## The updater

- An update would replace the boot image and remove root. On the first rooted
  run the script runs `pm hide com.amazon.device.software.ota`, then reads
  `hidden=true` back from `dumpsys package`.

## Back to stock: the stock target

`firebreak stock <build>` returns a Dot on amonet v1.1.0 or v2.0.0 to stock Fire
OS 6, to test a root from a clean start. `stock` is a target like the others, so
every state has a route to it: a rooted Dot goes to recovery, amonet v1.1.0's
fastboot gets amonet v1.1.0's TWRP, Fire OS 5's locked fastboot gets the
fastbrick, and in any TWRP the restore runs.

- Only `stock` takes a `BUILD`, and without one it installs 8149. The run
  downloads the build before it touches the Dot.
- The restore ends at stock-booted: the run waits for stock to start, which
  shows as `adb` answering `unauthorized` about 1.5 minutes after the reboot. A
  Dot already stock-booted, or in stock fastboot, has nothing to restore. An
  amonet v2.0.0-unlocked Dot booted into Fire OS 6 without root reads the same.
  So the message says "appears", then names the + gesture, which starts v2.0.0's
  TWRP, for a Dot unlocked with amonet v2.0.0. The condition comes first, so the
  owner of a plain stock Dot can skip it.
- The restore clears boot0 first and writes it back last. It creates
  `boot0-erased` before the clear and deletes it once boot0 reads back, as the
  downgrade does. So a restore stopped in between, then rebooted, is resumed
  through amonet v1.1.0's bootrom step and amonet v1.1.0's TWRP, and restored
  again. Before the marker, `stock` could not resume that Dot: it needed a
  TWRP, and the Dot showed up only as its bootrom.
- A resume clears the TWRP states from the run's acted-on set, so a run
  that started in TWRP acts on TWRP again after the bootrom step.
- Measured on a Dot, 2026-10-04: a restore stopped during the system write,
  then `adb reboot` from TWRP, then a rerun about 20 s later. The bootrom's one
  window was open when the rerun's bootrom step started, and amonet then
  skipped ports already present. The window closed, and nothing showed on USB
  for 9.5 minutes, until the cable was unplugged and plugged back in. The
  bootrom step then found it, and the run went on through amonet v1.1.0's TWRP
  and the whole restore to stock 8146: `[1/19]` to `[19/19]`, 13 min 50 s with
  the wait. The resume now takes a bootrom port already present, and asks for
  the replug only if nothing happens within a minute.
- A restore stopped without a reboot leaves TWRP running, so a rerun restores
  from there.
- The wait for stock to start has the 10-minute limit of any other state.
- A booted Fire OS 5 Dot without root gets `adb reboot recovery`, as the
  `stock` subcommand did. For the other targets that state is a boot still
  in progress, and the run waits.
- v2.0.0's TWRP mounts by name, under `/dev/block/platform/.../by-name/`, so an
  unmount pattern anchored on `/dev/block/mmcblk0` matched nothing there and
  the writes went to mounted filesystems. What is left mounted is named
  back, because a refused `umount` is otherwise silent and the Dot has no
  screen to look at.
- That TWRP carries two `dd` implementations which do not take the same
  operands: the one on the path answers `conv option disabled`. So the script
  uses `toybox dd` where it exists. TWRP 3.7.0_9-bboe1's `toybox dd`
  truncates at its `seek` offset, and the block device answers `ftruncate:
  Invalid argument`. So the one write with `seek` passes `conv=notrunc` to
  `toybox dd`.
- Measured on a Dot, 2026-10-04, before `stock` was a target: `stock 8146` from
  amonet-v1.1.0-booted, through v1.1.0's TWRP 3.2.3, in 6 min 9 s; from
  amonet-v2.0.0-booted, through v2.0.0's TWRP, in 2 min 59 s; from
  amonet-v1.1.0-bboe-booted in 1 min 52 s. Each was rooted again after the
  fastboot gesture: to `amonet-biscuit-v2.0.0` in 7 steps,
  `amonet-biscuit-v1.1.0` in 10, `amonet-biscuit-v1.1.0-bboe` in 9, as the
  table counts.
- As the target: from amonet-v1.1.0-bboe-booted in 3 min 11 s, `[1/17]` to
  `[17/17]`, ending when stock started, and 4315 in 4 min 9 s, which rooted
  back in 9 min 8 s. Run again on the stock Dot, it reported nothing to restore
  and downloaded nothing.
- `sgdisk`, `mke2fs`, `blockdev` and `md5sum` are looked for before the
  countdown, because `sgdisk` and `mke2fs` are not reached until after 1.6 GB
  has gone in.
- 4315 ships a block OTA in place of a `payload.bin`: `system.new.dat`
  with a transfer list, and boot, LK, TZ and the preloader as files in the
  zip. `system.img` is built from the list, which must be version 3 or 4
  and use only `erase`, `zero` and `new`, since the rest patch blocks this
  does not have. Its ranges must cover the image from 0, and no block may
  be written twice: Fire OS 5's own list erases the whole image and then
  writes over it, where 4315's erases only what it does not write. Only
  the `new` ranges are written, because the file is truncated to size
  first, and `system.new.dat` must end where the last of them does. A block OTA
  carries no hash per image, so they rest on the OTA's pinned SHA-256.
- LK, TZ and boot are padded to a multiple of 4096 bytes. A write is
  checked over whole sectors, `n // 512` of them, and 4315's LK is 241,448
  bytes, so the check left the tail out and failed on a write that had
  gone in. A payload build's images are already whole blocks.
- 4315's package number, 8087722874, is not the one the system it installs
  reports, 8087722884. The table's is the package's, which is what names
  the download.
- FTVDB keys an OTA by its md5, so the download is found by md5 and then
  pinned by the SHA-256 of a copy that matched it.
- Each `payload.bin` operation is read where it lies, so peak memory is one
  image plus one operation: about 1 GB, for the 768 MB system image.
- The manifest's own lengths are not trusted. A number cut short raised a bare
  `IndexError`, and a field cut short returned fewer bytes than it declared
  with no error, so the run ended in a traceback or wrote a short image. Both
  now stop with the reason. A field read as the wrong wire type stops too: it
  used to reach an attribute that an int does not have.
- A passed check prints `ok` rather than the tick where the console cannot
  encode it, such as a legacy Windows code page, which would otherwise raise
  `UnicodeEncodeError`.

### The partition table

- amonet v1.1.0 renames the stock `boot_a` and `boot_b` to `boot_a_x` and
  `boot_b_x`, and adds its own `boot_a` and `boot_b` as partitions 17 and 18,
  cut from the end of userdata. The stock table is rebuilt from the Dot's own
  rather than from constants, so every offset and size is that Dot's.
- The backup table goes in before the primary: a run cut during the primary's
  write leaves it mixed, and the backup is what the next run reads instead.
- A table with no `_x` name is already stock and is kept: that is what makes a
  stopped run safe to repeat, and why v2.0.0's table needs no surgery.
- The kernel must reread the table before cache and userdata are formatted.
  Against the old one, `mke2fs` would size userdata to amonet's cut.
- The first run on a Dot saves that Dot's own table as
  `current-gpt-<serial>.bin`, and later runs keep it rather than saving the
  stock table over it.

### The writes

- boot0's header is cleared first. With no valid preloader the bootrom waits
  for a USB host instead of running anything, so a failure anywhere after it
  leaves a Dot that amonet's bootrom step can reach without opening the case.
  Forced on hardware: the bootrom appeared 4 seconds after the reboot and
  waited about 39 seconds, and amonet v2.0.0's bootrom step rewrote the whole
  chain in 22. The order of the bootchain writes is not what makes this
  survivable -- with the header gone, no slot is reachable anyway -- the
  bootrom is.
- `misc` gets zeros and, at 0x360, the boot control block amonet v1.1.0's
  bootrom step writes: `00 41 42 42 01 8f 00`. Slot a is then `prio 15 tries 0
  success 1`, and slot b is unused. Each slot byte packs `priority:4`,
  `tries:3` and `success:1`, low bits first.
- A zeroed `misc` is not safe. Stock's first boot starts slot a with a few
  tries and `success 0`, and each boot cut short spends one. On a Dot, a
  gesture during the first boot and the power-ons after it left slot a at
  `tries 0 success 0`. The preloader then reset about every 30 s with the ring
  dark, never starting LK, so neither the gesture nor `FACTFACT` reached
  fastboot. It did not fall back to slot b, whose tries stayed at 3. It took
  the eMMC short to get back. A slot marked good spends no tries.
- `tee1` and `tee2` are not slot-tied: every slot-tied partition takes a letter
  from `ro.boot.slot_suffix` and TEE takes digits, and amonet's bootrom step
  writes its LK to both slots unconditionally, but its TEE payload only to
  `tee1`. So they go backup first and
  primary last, rather than by slot.
- boot is padded with zeros to the size of `boot_a`, which both slots share, so
  no byte of amonet's boot image survives and the read-back covers the whole
  partition.
- The preloader is padded the same way, to boot0's size, which depends on the
  eMMC chip. [devices.md](devices.md#emmc-chips) has the sizes.
- TWRP answers `ro.product.device` only because amonet's TWRP sets it in its
  `default.prop`; a recovery ramdisk has no reason to carry it otherwise.
- A push and its read-back address different block devices, whose page caches
  are not coherent, so the cache drop between them is what makes the comparison
  mean anything.
- `adb push` to a node that does not exist does not fail: adbd creates a
  regular file in TWRP's RAM-backed `/dev` and reports success. A partition's
  read-back still catches that, because it reads the raw disk at the computed
  offset rather than the node -- but only after 768 MB has gone into a tmpfs on
  a 512 MB device. So the start sector is checked first, which also catches a
  number that means a different partition in the table the kernel still holds.
- boot0 is the one write whose read-back names the node it wrote, so there a
  regular file would match itself and pass. Hence the check that it is a block
  device. For the same reason the cleared header is counted twice, bytes read
  and bytes left non-zero: a read that returned nothing would otherwise look
  like a header that cleared.
- The skip compares a mebibyte of head first, which costs 0.07 s and is enough
  because a different build differs within a kibibyte or two. Only a partition
  that looks right pays for the full comparison, 13 s against the 80 s a write
  would take.
- A restored Dot has no Wi-Fi until it is set up in the Alexa app. To root it
  again, skip that setup: on Wi-Fi it can update to a build firebreak does not
  support, or away from the build under test.

```

`docs/simulator.md`:

```md
# The simulator

`simulator/` runs firebreak against a simulated Dot. No Dot, real `adb`, real
`fastboot` or real serial port takes part.

## Running it

```sh
simulator/run.sh OUT amonet-biscuit-v1.1.0-bboe
START=no-light simulator/run.sh OUT amonet-biscuit-v1.1.0-bboe --short
python3 simulator/trace.py OUT.jsonl             # one command per line, for diff
python3 simulator/trace.py --commands OUT.jsonl  # with the payload commands per range
```

- `run.sh` makes a new Dot, runs firebreak, and leaves `OUT.log`, the trace
  (`OUT.jsonl` and `OUT.txt`), a disk snapshot (`OUT.snapshot`) and the raw
  partition table (`OUT.table`: LBA 0 to 33, then the last 33 sectors).
- `run.sh` destroys the Dot when it exits, also on Ctrl-C or `SIGTERM`. First
  it stops firebreak and the loop that waits to plug the Dot in, and waits for
  both. A `plug` in progress runs to its end. A Dot made with
  `python3 simulator/dot.py create` stays until `python3 simulator/dot.py destroy`.
- `run.sh` passes its pid to `create`. `create` refuses while the `run.sh`
  that the state names still runs.
- It needs Docker, `timeout` (coreutils, on macOS too), and the downloads in
  `~/.cache/firebreak`. The image build downloads busybox, pigz and toybox
  source and checks each by SHA-256.
- firebreak's cache is `~/.cache/firebreak-sim-cache`. `dot.py create` copies
  each download into its `firebreak` folder, as an APFS clone on macOS and
  with `--reflink=auto` elsewhere. Where a clone fails, as off APFS, it copies
  the file. So a run that writes a file in place changes only the copy. A copy
  whose size or modification time is not its download's is copied again. What
  a run builds there stays for the next run.
- State and the trace are in `~/.cache/firebreak-sim`. `FIREBREAK_SIM` and
  `FIREBREAK_SIM_CACHE` move the two, so several Dots can run at once. Each
  Dot needs its own pair: firebreak keeps its boot0-erased marker in the
  cache.
- `create` refuses a `FIREBREAK_SIM_CACHE`, or a `firebreak` folder in it,
  that is the same directory as `~/.cache` or `~/.cache/firebreak`, or as
  `$XDG_CACHE_HOME` or its `firebreak` folder. It compares device and inode,
  so another case, a link, a trailing slash or a relative path does not get
  past it. It also refuses when `HOME` is not set, and when the `firebreak`
  folder is a link. firebreak would otherwise write the real cache.
- `START=fastboot`, the default, starts in stock fastboot. `START=no-light`
  erases boot0 and leaves the Dot unplugged. `run.sh` plugs it in when
  firebreak prints "Short the Dot's test point".
- firebreak's stdin is `/dev/null`. Its stdout must be unbuffered: without
  `PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1`, the prompt reached the log only at exit, and the Dot
  was never plugged in.
- `run.sh` stops if `adb` or `fastboot` on its PATH is not the simulator's, or
  if the virtualenv's port hook is not loaded.
- `simulator/bin/adb` and `simulator/bin/fastboot` run `dot.py` under the virtualenv's
  interpreter, and fail when there is none. Through pyenv's shim, `python3`
  took 270 ms to start against 14 ms, and a run makes up to about 260 calls.
- After firebreak exits, `run.sh` runs `dot.py halt` before it copies the
  trace. `halt` sends the bootrom SIGTERM, which writes its open range, and
  waits up to 10 s for it to exit. It signals the pid only when that process
  runs this checkout's `bootrom.py` for this state folder. Otherwise it drops
  the pid. The bootrom clears its pid from the state on every exit.
- `create` destroys the Dot that the state names before it makes a new one.
  `destroy` halts too, then takes the state's lock. It stops a bootrom that
  started after the halt, sets each of the Dot's loop devices writable,
  detaches it, and removes the container. It skips a loop whose backing
  inode, offset or size is no longer the Dot's.
- A stopped container cannot run the detach, and its loops stay bound to the
  deleted disk files. So `destroy` starts a stopped container first. If the
  detach still fails, it copies the loop records out and runs the detach in a
  new privileged container of the same image, with the same checks. If that
  fails too, or the container is gone, it prints a warning with the records.

## What runs for real

- The eMMC is files in a privileged container: the user area, boot0, boot1
  and the RPMB. `sim-boot` makes a loop device for each table entry at each
  boot.
- The container has no network (`--network none`). firebreak itself runs on
  the host and keeps its network.
- The container is privileged. So it sees the Docker VM's devices under
  Docker Desktop, and the host's devices on a Linux host. At each boot
  `sim-boot` removes every block node from the container's `/dev` and then
  makes nodes for its own loops only. `/dev` is the container's tmpfs, so
  this removes nothing on the host. It takes loop numbers from
  `/dev/loop-control`, so any number of Dots can run.
- At each boot `sim-boot` kills every process that the previous boot's
  commands left running, a leftover `sleep infinity` too. A Dot does not keep
  writing across a reboot. It spares pid 1, its oldest child (the container's
  command) and `sim-boot`, by pid. It spares the loop holder only while that
  pid runs `sim-hold` for this run folder.
- Every `adb shell` and `exec-out` command runs in the container as the uid
  and gid that the Dot's adbd gives it, under that environment's `sh`. Its
  `PATH` holds only that environment's commands. No GNU tool is on it, except
  bash where TWRP ships bash.

| environment | `sh` | commands | uid |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| TWRP 3.2.3 | busybox | busybox; `gunzip`, `gzip` and `unpigz` are pigz | 0 |
| TWRP 3.7.0_9-0, 3.7.0_9-bboe2 | mksh | toybox; `dd`, `grep`, `awk`, `unzip`, `bash` and `unpigz` are their own | 0 |
| Fire OS 5 | mksh | toolbox's names, `busybox`, `gzip`; no `md5sum` or `wc` by name; `su` under Magisk only | 2000 |
| Fire OS 6, root adb | mksh | toybox; `dd`, `ps`, `top` are toolbox's; `grep` and `gzip` are their own; no `gunzip` | 0 |

- Each list is read from the image. TWRP's is each ramdisk's `/sbin`. Fire OS
  5's is `/system/bin` of the image that the Fire OS 5 routes write
  (`system-6ababc517529`). Fire OS 6's is `/system/bin` of stock 8146.
- Every `/sbin` name that a TWRP list leaves out is in
  `/sim/flavours/*.missing`, as is every name that a Fire OS list maps to
  toolbox or toybox and the simulator does not have. Other Fire OS programs
  that no tool calls, such as `am`, `logcat` and `svc`, are not listed.
- Fire OS 5 has no toybox. Its `/system/bin/busybox` is BusyBox v1.22.1,
  static, with 354 applets, and no name links to it. So `md5sum`, `wc`,
  `gunzip` and `zcat` work only as `busybox md5sum` and so on.
- The builds are the closest that run in the container, not the Dot's
  binaries:

| on the Dot | in the simulator |
| --- | --- |
| busybox 1.22.1, bionic | busybox 1.22.1, glibc, `defconfig` |
| pigz 2.2.5 | pigz 2.2.5 |
| toybox 0.7.6, Android's config | toybox 0.7.6, `defconfig` with `dd`, `gzip`, `gunzip`, `zcat`, `tr` and `expr`, on zlib |
| mksh, Android's | mksh R59c |
| toolbox's `dd`, and 3.7.0's own | `toolbox-dd`: refuses `conv=` with `dd: conv option disabled`, else runs toybox `dd` |
| 3.7.0's and Fire OS 6's own `grep` | toybox `grep` |
| 3.7.0's own `awk` | original-awk 2023-11-27 |
| 3.7.0's own `unzip` | Info-ZIP unzip 6.0 |
| Fire OS 5's busybox 1.22.1, static | the busybox above: the 354 applet names match |
| Fire OS 5's and 6's `gzip` (zlib's `minigzip`) | `minigzip` from zlib 1.2.8 |
| Fire OS 5's toolbox, Fire OS 6's `ps` and `top` | toybox 0.7.6 under toolbox's names |
| toolbox `md5` and `hd` | `simulator/device/toolbox`: AOSP 5.1's `md5.c` and `hd.c` in Python |
| toolbox `start`, `stop` and `log` | no-ops that exit 0 |
| toolbox `getevent` | waits for ever: there is no input device |
| `sgdisk`, `mke2fs`, `e2fsck`, `resize2fs`, `tune2fs` | gdisk 1.0.10, e2fsprogs 1.47.0 |

- The base image is `ubuntu:24.04` pinned by digest. The image build gets
  these packages, not pinned: cpio 2.15+dfsg-1ubuntu2.1, e2fsprogs
  1.47.0-2.4~exp1ubuntu4.1, gdisk 1.0.10-1build1, mksh 59c-37, original-awk
  2023-11-27-1, python3 3.12.3-0ubuntu2.1, python3-fusepy 3.0.1-5, unzip
  6.0-28ubuntu4.1 and util-linux 2.39.3-9ubuntu6.6, as built on 2026-10-11.
- `minigzip -d` passes input with no gzip magic through, as zlib's `gzread`
  does, and exits 0.

- Names that a ramdisk links but the simulator's build lacks are left out.
  The image lists them in `/sim/flavours/*.missing`: mostly SELinux commands,
  `test` and `[` outside the shell, `tar`, `diff`, `stty` and the SHA-2 sums.
- toybox 0.7.6's `gunzip` passes input with no gzip magic through, as `cat`
  does, and exits 0. A stream cut short exits 1 with `gzclose: Illegal
  seek`. pigz 2.2.5 refuses input with no magic, `not compressed --
  skipping`, and exits 2.
- toybox `dd` truncates its output at `seek`, and a block device answers
  `ftruncate: Invalid argument`. toolbox's `dd` writes a block device whole,
  as overdub's rooting.md measured. So `toolbox-dd` adds `conv=notrunc` when
  the output is a block device.
- glibc's `realpath` fails on a missing last component, so busybox
  `readlink -f` prints nothing for it. Bionic's prints the path, which
  `fix-symlinks.sh` relies on. The simulator models that script's result
  rather than running it.
- `/data/local/tmp` is `root:shell` `0771` under Fire OS 5, as overdub's
  pitfalls measured. A push there lands `0666`. Elsewhere a push lands as
  the container's `umask` leaves it.
- Device nodes are `root` `0600`, so uid 2000 can neither read nor write
  them. `/proc/sys/vm/drop_caches` and `force_ro` refuse uid 2000 too. These
  modes are Linux's and Android's defaults, not read from a Dot.
- Each boot is decided from the disk: the preloader in boot0, the LK in
  `lk_a`, the TWRP in `recovery`, the markers in `expdb` and `misc`, the build
  in `system_X`, and Magisk in `userdata`.

## What is modelled

- `adb`, `fastboot`, `getprop`, `setprop`, `su`, `id` under Fire OS, `bcbtool`,
  `pm`, `dumpsys`, `getenforce` and `reboot` are stubs. `twrp install` writes a
  Fire OS 6 build for an OTA zip and marks root adb for any other zip.
- `su` is a setuid binary. It gives root only while Magisk's database is in
  `userdata`, and Fire OS 5 has it on `PATH` only then. Magisk's busybox is not
  put first on `PATH` under `su`.
- `su` reads its words with `getopt_long`, the options and the option string
  of Magisk 17.3's `native/jni/su/su.c` (tag `v17.3`). As there, getopt moves
  the words that are not options to the end. `-c` takes the command from the
  word before `optind`, so `su 0 -c id x` runs `id x` and `su -cid` runs
  `-cid`. The first word left after the options, and after a `-`, is the
  user. So `su 0 -c` and `su root -c` set the uid. `-cn` and `-mm` are
  rewritten to `-z` and `-M`, and `-u` prints the usage and exits 2.
- Not as Magisk: glibc's `getopt_long`, where Magisk has bionic's; names
  other than `root`, `system` and `shell` read as a number, where bionic
  knows every Android id; and `-s`, `-l`, `-p` and `-M` change nothing. Its
  own PTY, which Magisk opens when stdin is a terminal, is not modelled. It
  exits with the command's status.
- Under uid 2000, `setprop` sets only `debug.*`, `log.*`,
  `service.adb.root`, `service.adb.tcp.port` and `sys.powerctl`, which AOSP
  5.1 lets the shell set. Any other name is ignored and `setprop` exits 0. A
  set `ro.` name does not change. `sys.powerctl` set to `reboot,X` reboots
  into X.
- `reboot` sets `sys.powerctl`, as 5.1's `reboot` does, so the shell can
  reboot. It returns at once, where 5.1's waits for the reboot.
- `/sdcard` and `/data/local/tmp` are container folders. They have no size
  limit, and a format of `userdata` does not empty them. `/sdcard` is `0777`:
  its FUSE modes are not modelled.
- boot0 holds a preloader when it starts `EMMC_BOOT` and holds `BRLYT` at
  0x200. Otherwise the Dot boots into the bootrom. So amonet v1.1.0's
  `EMMC_BOOT` and zeros, and firebreak's zeros, both reach it. Nothing else in
  boot0 is checked: not the preloader's own header or its signature, and not
  boot1.
- `lk_b`, `tee1` and `tee2` take no part in a boot. Only `lk_a` decides the
  LK.
- The fastbrick writes amonet v2.0.0's chain directly. It runs no exploit.
- amonet v1.1.0's LK clears `FASTBOOT_PLEASE` in `expdb` when it acts on it.
  This is inferred: `fastboot-step.sh` reaches TWRP with the marker still
  written, so the marker cannot hold the Dot in fastboot.

### boot0 and boot1

- Their loop devices are read-only from the start of each boot, and again
  after each `fastboot flash` or `erase` of them.
- `/sys/block/mmcblk0bootN` is a FUSE file system with `force_ro` and `size`,
  over a tmpfs. A boot fails unless the FUSE file system is the top mount
  there. So a write cannot reach a Linux host's own `/sys/block`.
- A write to `force_ro` sets the loop device read-only or writable before it
  returns. So `echo 0` and `echo 1` hold for any writer: `dd`, `toybox dd`, or
  a shell redirect.
- The value is read as the Dot's kernel, Linux 3.18.19, reads it: with
  `simple_strtoul` in base 0. A number other than 0 sets read-only, and text
  with no number first is `EINVAL`. So `1_0` sets read-only, and `-1` and a
  space before `1` are `EINVAL`. Later kernels use `kstrtoul`, which refuses
  `1_0`.
- A read gives the loop device's flag. So after a direct `blockdev --setrw`
  it reads 0.
- Only root can write `force_ro`.

### TWRP

- Each TWRP replaces its by-name bootloader nodes at boot, as the script in
  its ramdisk does. A write through one does not reach the disk.
- 3.2.3 (`/sbin/fix-symlinks.sh`): `lk_a`, `lk_b`, `tee1` and `tee2` are
  `/dev/null`, and `X_real` names the partition. `boot_a` and `boot_b` name
  `boot_a_x` and `boot_b_x`, and `boot_X_amonet` names amonet's. With no
  `boot_X_x`, `boot_X` is a link to the missing `by-name/boot_X_x`.
- 3.7.0_9-0 and 3.7.0_9-bboe2 (`/sbin/slot-symlinks.sh`): `lk_a`, `lk_b`,
  `tee1`, `tee2`, `tee` and `preloader` are files in `/tmp/ota-decoy`, at
  1, 1, 5, 5, 5 and 4 MiB. `X_real` names each one that was a partition.
- `/tmp/ota-decoy` is a 64 MiB tmpfs, so a `dd` with no `count` there stops.
- bboe2 also points `boot_a` and `boot_b` at `boot_a_x` and `boot_b_x` when
  they exist, and keeps amonet's as `boot_X_amonet`.
- Not modelled: `/dev/block/other-lk`, `other-system` and `other-boot`,
  `/dev/block/current-boot` and `current-system`, `/dev/block/bootdevice`,
  `/dev/block/by-name` and `/dev/block/platform/bootdevice`. A command that
  names one finds nothing.
- adb answers 5 s before MTP is on. Until then `sys.usb.config` is `adb` and
  `ro.twrp.version` is not set. The 5 s is from the ring's timing in
  rooting.md.
- The switch to `mtp,adb` drops USB for 1 s. A call made then fails with
  adb's error text, and `adb devices` lists nothing. A shell call running
  across the switch runs to its end, prints the first half of its output
  and exits 255, or 0 without the shell protocol. A push fails with `failed
  to read copy response: EOF`.
  The trace marks each one `# cut by the USB switch`. None of this is
  measured: not the 1 s, the command running on, the half, the status or the
  text.

### adb

- The `adbd` of TWRP 3.2.3 (5.1.1, `LMY49M`) and of Fire OS 5.5.5.4 (from
  its OTA's `boot.img`) list no `shell_v2` feature and hold
  `create_subproc_pty`. The `adbd` of both 3.7.0 TWRPs and of Fire OS 6 list
  `shell_v2`. Each was read from its image.
- So under 3.2.3 and Fire OS 5, adb has no shell protocol. adb 37's client
  (`client/commandline.cpp`) then:
  - exits 0 from `adb shell` whatever the command did, and 1 only when it
    cannot connect;
  - never tells the Dot that stdin ended, so a command that reads stdin to
    its end never ends;
  - refuses a service string over 4096 bytes with `error: shell command too
    long`, and `-T` with `error: device only supports allocating a pty`.
- 5.1's `adbd` (`adb/services.c`) runs a `shell:` command on a PTY, which it
  leaves in the kernel's default mode, and an `exec:` command on a socket
  pair. So the output has CR LF, and the line discipline acts on stdin: it
  echoes it, edits lines, and sends `SIGINT`, `SIGQUIT` and `SIGTSTP` for
  0x03, 0x1c and 0x1a. `sim-pty` runs the command so, on a PTY in the
  container with the container kernel's default mode.
- That PTY is the container's kernel, Linux 6.x, not the Dot's 3.18.19. It
  passes all output that the command wrote before the PTY closes. The Dot's
  3.18 kernel and Lollipop's `adbd` can lose the tail of the output. The
  simulator never loses it, and models no such loss.
- When the host's `adb` exits, `sim-pty` hangs the PTY up, as `adbd` does
  when it closes the PTY. That `adbd` closes it then is inferred.
- `exec-out` exits 0 in every environment: the client never reads a status.
  The command's stdin never ends there either.
- With the shell protocol, under 3.7.0_9-0, bboe2 and Fire OS 6, `adb shell`
  passes the status, as overdub's rooting.md measured for 3.7.0. `-n` ends
  the command's stdin at once. Otherwise the shim passes stdin and then ends
  it.
- adb's own options are read only before the command, as adb's `getopt`
  with `+` does: `adb shell ls -t` runs `ls -t`.
- 3.2.3's `__bionic_open_tzdata` lines are not modelled. Nor is what `-x`
  makes a protocol `adbd` do.

### fastboot

- A locked LK refuses `flash` and `erase` with `the command you input is
  restricted on locked hw`, which a locked v1.1.0 LK printed.
- `flash` of an image larger than its partition fails with `size too large`.
  `flash` or `erase` of a name that is not in the table, nor boot0 or boot1,
  fails with `unknown partition`. Neither text is measured.
- `-S` sets the size above which `flash` reports `Sending sparse`, with the
  count of pieces. The write is the same either way. Without `-S` an image is
  sent whole.
- `oem reboot-recovery` reboots into recovery on any LK, locked or not. That
  a locked LK accepts it is not measured.

### The bootrom

- The bootrom is `simulator/bootrom.py`, on a pseudo-terminal, started at the boot
  into the bootrom. It runs no code. It answers the handshake, `0xd1`,
  `0xd4` and `0xc8`.
- The crypto engine at `0x10210000` answers function 126 only after the
  acquire words, with length 1 and slot pointers 18, 26, 26. It then writes
  the input words XOR the zero decryption to the destination.
- Before `0x102868` holds the range-check pattern, word access below
  `0x10000000` is refused. So a payload upload or a jump without the defeat
  fails.
- A write to `0x1028A8` jumps. The bootrom compares the memory at `0x201000`
  with the `payload.bin` of each amonet zip. It answers as the payload that
  matches, and stays silent for any other code.

### The payload

- The payload speaks its own command set: v1.1.0's six commands, or the
  twelve that v2.0.0's client sends.
- `0x5002` sends v2.0.0's 512-byte test pattern, so its client's fast-send
  check passes. `0x5003` answers the acknowledgement.
- Block commands go to the disk files through `sim-emmc` in the container.
  The RPMB is the first 256 bytes of its file. A new Dot's RPMB starts
  `AMZN`.
- `0x1003` and `0x1004` take 1 to 64 blocks, as amonet's client allows. A
  count outside that, a read or write past the end of its area, or any
  access to an area other than 0, 1 and 2 halts the payload: it logs
  `halted` and answers nothing more. What the real payload does there is not
  known.
- `0x5000` reads any address at any length. Whether the real payload bounds
  either is not known.
- The `0x3000` reboot hands the Dot back to the boot rules, 3 s later.
- The port is not modelled: the short, the watchdog, the preloader's
  `0e8d:2000` port and USB packet sizes do not exist here.

### Leniencies

The simulator accepts these, where a real Dot may not:

- a jump without the cache-disable command (`0xC8 0xB1`)
- a crypto engine that ignores SOURCE and does not need the key and IV slots
  cleared
- root on Fire OS 5 decided by `magisk.db` alone
- amonet v2.0.0's LK reading unlocked whatever the TEE holds
- any LK but stock booting any known TWRP
- the fastbrick on an unlocked LK
- the fastbrick leaving boot0 as it was; the real one writes the preloader
- `-s` and `ANDROID_SERIAL`, which it records and ignores
- `dd` past 2 GiB of `mmcblk0` under bboe2 and 3.7.0_9-0, where the Dot's
  `dd` answers `EINVAL`

## The port

- `dot.py create` makes a virtualenv in `~/.cache/firebreak-sim/venv`. A
  `.pth` file there imports `ports.py` at every start, so the children that a
  tool starts with `sys.executable` see the port too, whatever their
  `PYTHONPATH`.
- `ports.py` replaces `serial.tools.list_ports.comports` when pyserial loads.
  It lists only the simulated bootrom, `0e8d:0003`, and only while the Dot is
  in its bootrom. It never lists a real port.
- Under Python 3.9, pyserial from the wheel loads through `zipimporter`, which
  has no `exec_module`. So the hook runs the module's code from `get_code`.
- The bootrom keeps the pseudo-terminal's other end open, so a tool can close
  and reopen the port.

## The clock

- `FIREBREAK_SIM_SPEED` sets the speed, K. `run.sh` sets 20 when it is not
  set. At 1 or less the hook changes nothing.
- The hook replaces `time.monotonic`, `time.perf_counter`, `time.time`, their
  `_ns` forms, `time.sleep`, `select.select` and the `select` method of each
  `selectors` class. It does this in every process the virtualenv's
  interpreter starts: the tool, its children, `dot.py` and `bootrom.py`.
  `run.sh` runs `plug`, `halt`, `snapshot` and `table` under that interpreter
  too.
- The clock runs K times faster than real time, except while a process holds
  it. Then it runs at real time in every process. A `dot.py` call holds it
  from its start to its exit, except while it sleeps. `bootrom.py` holds it
  for each eMMC access. So work in the container takes real time, and so
  does a tool timeout that waits for it.
- `clock.json`, in the state folder, holds the anchor in real and in clock
  time, the offset to wall time, and the pids that hold the clock. A change
  replaces the file whole, under `clock.lock`. Each change drops the pids that
  are gone, so a call that a tool kills holds the clock only until the next
  change. `create` deletes the file.
- Each trace line's `time` is the clock's, not real time, except the lines
  that `create` writes: `run.sh` runs it without the hook.
- A sleep or a wait for a descriptor sleeps at most 50 ms of real time at
  once, so a change of speed takes effect within 50 ms.
- pyserial keeps `time.monotonic` as a class attribute and calls it through
  `self`. A plain function there gets `self` as an argument, so the clock's
  functions are bound methods.

These stay in real time:

- `Event.wait`, `Lock.acquire` and `Condition.wait` with a timeout. firebreak
  uses them only for its progress line, every 0.1 s, and for its cache lock,
  in a loop with no deadline.
- Socket timeouts. Only downloads use them, and the cache holds every
  download.
- `time.strftime` in firebreak's log. It only prints.
- `run.sh`: `timeout 1500`, and the 0.5 s poll for the short prompt.

Work outside a hold counts K times: the interpreter's start before the hook,
firebreak's own work, and the pseudo-terminal. With 6 Dots at once a call to
`adb` or `fastboot` spends about 100 ms there, 2 s at K=20. These timeouts
bound K:

- firebreak kills `fastboot flash brick` after 8 s, as a Dot needs. At K=20,
  with 6 Dots at once, the call took 1.6 to 2.8 s to start and the chain 0.8
  to 1.3 s more. At K=100, 1 of the 3 runs from stock fastboot was killed
  before the reboot, and at K=200 all 3 were. Each then stopped with "the Dot
  has been stock-fastboot for 10 minutes".
- firebreak waits 5 s for a 64-block payload read. At K=100 and at K=200, 1
  of the 3 runs from no light stopped there, with 25,550 and 26,572 of
  32,772 bytes.
- At K=20 all 18 runs rooted, 6 at a time. At K=100, 2 of 6 stopped, and at
  K=200, 4 of 6.

### Measured

Measured on 2026-10-12, firebreak to each target from no light with `--short`
and from stock fastboot, 6 Dots at once. Each Dot had its own `FIREBREAK_SIM`
and `FIREBREAK_SIM_CACHE`. A time is from the start of `run.sh` to its
summary line, and includes `create` and `snapshot`.

| run | K=1 | calls | K=20 | calls | K=20 | calls |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| v1-bboe, no light | 73 s | 181 | 60 s | 165 | 58 s | 165 |
| v1, no light | 75 s | 191 | 58 s | 175 | 60 s | 175 |
| v2, no light | 103 s | 275 | 60 s | 249 | 60 s | 248 |
| v1-bboe, fastboot | 87 s | 186 | 64 s | 164 | 64 s | 164 |
| v1, fastboot | 87 s | 186 | 64 s | 164 | 64 s | 164 |
| v2, fastboot | 63 s | 65 | 30 s | 51 | 28 s | 51 |

- The 6 runs took 103 s at K=1 and 64 s at K=20, both times.
- A run makes fewer calls at a higher K. A call's start counts K times, so
  fewer polls fit in a wait.
- At K=20 and K=100, each run's trace, snapshot and raw table matched the run
  at K=1, but for the number of polls.

## The trace

- Every `adb` and `fastboot` call, boot and reboot goes to `trace.jsonl`, one
  locked write per line, with the whole command line, global flags included.
  Each call goes in when it starts, so a call that a tool kills still shows.
  A push and a `fastboot flash` carry the file's md5.
- A shell call that passes stdin adds a second line when it ends, `# stdin
  sent BYTES:MD5 received BYTES:MD5: COMMAND` in `trace.py`'s output.
- `sent` counts the bytes that the host's shim wrote into `docker exec`.
  `received` counts the bytes that arrived in the container. `sim-stdin`
  counts them there, between `docker exec` and the command's `sh` or
  `sim-pty`, and passes them on through a pipe. Neither counts what the
  command read. `received unknown` means `sim-stdin` did not report, as when
  a reboot killed it.
- When the command exits before it reads all of its stdin, `received` can be
  less than `sent`: the rest is still in Docker. When the command reads to
  its end, a `received` less than `sent` is bytes that Docker lost.
- The bootrom logs each command with its address and words, and a refused
  `read32` or `write32` as `refused` with its count. An upload of more than 16
  words logs its count and md5.
- The payload logs reads and writes as an area, a first block, a count and
  the md5 of the bytes. Contiguous reads, or contiguous writes, join into one
  range. `--commands` shows the commands each range took. It also shows the
  watchdog kicks and cache reads sent between ranges, as `between ranges`.
- `trace.py` puts `$CACHE` for the simulator's cache folders and
  `~/.cache/firebreak`, and `$TMP` for temporary names. It drops `adb
  devices`, `adb get-state` and `fastboot devices`; `--keep-polls` keeps
  them.
- `adb shell -n getprop NAME`, `fastboot getvar NAME` or `adb wait-for-X`,
  the whole line and nothing more, is not printed again when it is the same
  as the last line printed. A dropped poll between them does not count, so
  the number of polls before TWRP is up does not show. Every other line is
  printed each time, compound commands included, so a command sent twice
  shows twice.

## Traps

- `dd if=/dev/zero` onto a regular file with no `count` does not stop at the
  file's end. One filled Docker's disk. The container was gone, but its loop
  devices still held the deleted file, so the space came back only after a
  Docker restart.
- A loop that `losetup` attached and that then fails its backing check, or
  its `blockdev`, is detached before the boot fails. A loop whose backing
  inode is not the Dot's is left alone.
- A loop device's read-only flag outlives `losetup -d`. A loop that was
  boot1 at one boot came back as `boot_b` at the next and refused every write
  with `Operation not permitted`. Loop numbers are shared by every container
  on the host, too. So `sim-boot` sets each loop writable when it attaches
  it and before it detaches it, and `destroy` does the same.
- A container removed without `destroy` leaves its loop devices attached in
  the Docker VM. `losetup -a` in any privileged container lists them.
- busybox and toybox `umount` free the loop device under what they unmount,
  where on a Dot `LOOP_CLR_FD` fails on an eMMC partition. Freed, the loop
  number went to another Dot's partition: one Dot's `sgdisk` then read a
  768 MiB disk with no table, or a disk of 0 sectors, and one snapshot read
  `system_a` through `rpmb`. So `sim-hold` keeps every loop open for the boot,
  and the kernel only marks a busy loop to clear when it closes.
- `sim-hold` writes to `/sim/run/holder.log`. A boot fails unless it holds
  every loop. `facts` and `snapshot` fail unless it still runs.
- FUSE 2 refuses a mount point that is not empty, so `/sys/block` is mounted
  with `nonempty`.
- `docker exec -u 2000` runs with gid 0, so a `root:shell` `0771` folder
  refuses it. The simulator passes `2000:2000`.
- The ext4 file systems differ on every run. The snapshot compares their file
  trees, not their bytes.

```

`firebreak/__init__.py`:

```py
__version__ = "0.2.0"
